What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. It’s like I finish one thing—whether it's at school with my students or just stuff around the house—and I immediately jump right into the next thing. I just can't ever seem to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still or feel restless when you're supposed to be staying in one place?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I have to sit through an IEP meeting for one of my students, or if I'm in one of my admin classes, I just can't stay still. My leg will be bouncing under the table, or I'm clicking a pen... just shifting around in my chair constantly. I actually do it so much that people will glance over at me, and half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until I see them looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. Um... I mean, I know I'm always moving and can't sit still, but I don't really make snap decisions or do things without thinking them through first. I mean, I'm pretty thorough with my work, especially with my students' paperwork. I don't really struggle with missing details or making careless mistakes like that. My issue is more just the constant need to be moving, not so much my focus or attention to the actual work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It happens a lot at work. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting, and I always tell myself I'm going to be brief. But then... I just keep going. I'll hear myself giving, you know, three different examples, a story about something that happened in my classroom, and just throwing out ideas that nobody even asked for. And I can see that the other person is clearly trying to wrap things up, but I just talk right through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes quickly or frequently?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My mood is pretty stable, I'd say. Um, I mean, I get stressed or frustrated sometimes just from the job, but I don't have sudden mood swings or quick changes like that. My issue is more just the constant need to be moving, not so much my focus or attention to the actual work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. It’s like I finish one thing—whether it's at school with my students or just stuff around the house—and I immediately jump right into the next thing. I just can't ever seem to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still or feel restless when you're supposed to be staying in one place?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I have to sit through an IEP meeting for one of my students, or if I'm in one of my admin classes, I just can't stay still. My leg will be bouncing under the table, or I'm clicking a pen... just shifting around in my chair constantly. I actually do it so much that people will glance over at me, and half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until I see them looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. Um... I mean, I know I'm always moving and can't sit still, but I don't really make snap decisions or do things without thinking them through first. I haven't really noticed that being an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty thorough with my work, especially with my students' paperwork. I don't really struggle with missing details or making careless mistakes like that. My issue is more just the constant need to be moving, not so much my focus or attention to the actual work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It happens a lot at work. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting, and I always tell myself I'm going to be brief. But then... I just keep going. I'll hear myself giving, you know, three different examples, a story about something that happened in my classroom, and just throwing out ideas that nobody even asked for. And I can see that the other person is clearly trying to wrap things up, but I just talk right through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes quickly or frequently?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My mood is pretty stable, I'd say. Um, I mean, I get stressed or frustrated sometimes just from the job, but I don't have sudden mood swings or quick changes like that. I'm generally pretty even-keeled emotionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, working in a special ed classroom, there's always a lot going on—kids making noise, things moving around, constant activity. But it doesn't really pull my focus away. I can tune it out pretty well. \n\nIt's more... well, like I mentioned before, my issue is really just my own motor constantly running, rather than getting sidetracked by whatever is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's causing major problems, honestly. At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that administration actually put me on a corrective plan. And at home... well, my spouse says that our normal caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else that needs to get done. \n\nIt's tough to admit, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help them out, but it's clearly having the opposite effect.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Um, my mother actually used to joke that even back in elementary school, I taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. I mean... I haven't really noticed it being an issue with my friends. The problems are mostly just at work and at home, like I was saying. But as far as making or keeping friends... no, it doesn't really seem to get in the way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. And then at home, having my spouse tell me that they have to manage my energy on top of all our normal caregiving routines... it just feels terrible. So, yes, my quality of life is definitely suffering. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. Those are definitely where it causes the most problems. Like I said, being on that corrective plan at school, and then the strain it puts on my marriage. I can't really think of any other specific areas outside of those two. That's pretty much where it hits the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and constant activity are more related to an inability to stay still and calm, or to difficulties with focusing on one task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. Um... I guess I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It's hard to pull those two things apart. \n\nI just know that I can't seem to stay in one place or just relax. Whether that's because I can't focus on one task for very long, or if my body just physically can't sit still... I don't really know which one is driving the other. It just all kind of happens at once, so I couldn't really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are impacting your ability to enjoy leisure activities or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, just having quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me. If my spouse is trying to watch a show, or... or if my relative is resting, I just can't sit there quietly. I'll start narrating what I'm doing, or humming, or I'll just start moving things around the room. It usually keeps up until someone finally asks me to please settle down. So, yeah, enjoying any kind of quiet downtime or relaxing is pretty tough right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My mood is pretty stable, I'd say. Um, I mean, I get stressed or frustrated sometimes just from the job, but I don't have sudden mood swings or quick changes like that. I mean, working in a special ed classroom, there's always a lot going on—kids making noise, things moving around, constant activity. But it doesn't really pull my focus away. I can tune it out pretty well. \n\nIt's more... well, like I mentioned before, my issue is really just my own motor constantly running, rather than getting sidetracked by whatever is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's causing major problems, honestly. At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that administration actually put me on a corrective plan. And at home... well, my spouse says that our normal caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else that needs to get done. \n\nIt's tough to admit, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help them out, but it's clearly having the opposite effect.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Um, my mother actually used to joke that even back in elementary school, I taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this recent family change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your relationships with friends or making it hard for you to make and keep friends?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say so, no. I mean... I haven't really noticed it being an issue with my friends. The problems are mostly just at work and at home, like I was saying. But as far as making or keeping friends... no, it doesn't really seem to get in the way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um, like I was saying earlier, it's taking a pretty big toll on everything. \n\nWhen things get to the point at work where you're actually put on a corrective plan... that's incredibly stressful. And then at home, having my spouse tell me that they have to manage my energy on top of all our normal caregiving routines... it just feels terrible. So, yes, my quality of life is definitely suffering. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. Those are definitely where it causes the most problems. Like I said, being on that corrective plan at school, and then the strain it puts on my marriage. I can't really think of any other specific areas outside of those two. That's pretty much where it hits the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and constant activity are more related to an inability to stay still and calm, or to difficulties with focusing on one task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. Um... I guess I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It's hard to pull those two things apart. \n\nI just know that I can't seem to stay in one place or just relax. Whether that's because I can't focus on one task for very long, or if my body just physically can't sit still... I don't really know which one is driving the other. It just all kind of happens at once, so I couldn't really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are impacting your ability to enjoy leisure activities or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, just having quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me. If my spouse is trying to watch a show, or... or if my relative is resting, I just can't sit there quietly. I'll start narrating what I'm doing, or humming, or I'll just start moving things around the room. It usually keeps up until someone finally asks me to please settle down. So, yeah, enjoying any kind of quiet downtime or relaxing is pretty tough right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by your inability to control your constant activity and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel overwhelmed or distressed by it, no. I mean... it's definitely frustrating because of the problems it causes at work and with my wife, like I mentioned before. But the actual moving around itself doesn't distress me. \n\nIt's just kind of how I operate. I don't sit there feeling upset that I can't stop moving, I just... I just do it. It's more of an issue for the people around me than it is an overwhelming feeling for me personally. So, no, I wouldn't use the word distressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this recent family change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your relationships with friends or making it hard for you to make and keep friends?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say so, no. I mean... I haven't really noticed it being an issue with my friends. The problems are mostly just at work and at home, like I was saying. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. I can't really think of any other specific areas outside of those two. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help them out, but it's clearly having the opposite effect.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Um, my mother actually used to joke that even back in elementary school, I taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this recent family change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your relationships with friends or making it hard for you to make and keep friends?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say so, no. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. And I can see that the other person is clearly trying to wrap things up, but I just talk right through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes quickly or frequently?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My mood is pretty stable, I'd say. Um, I mean, I get stressed or frustrated sometimes just from the job, but I don't have sudden mood swings or quick changes like that. I'm generally pretty even-keeled emotionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, working in a special ed classroom, there's always a lot going on—kids making noise, things moving around, constant activity. But it doesn't really pull my focus away. I can tune it out pretty well. \n\nIt's more... well, like I mentioned before, my issue is really just my own motor constantly running, rather than getting sidetracked by whatever is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's causing major problems, honestly. At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that administration actually put me on a corrective plan. And at home... well, my spouse says that our normal caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else that needs to get done. \n\nIt's tough to admit, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help them out, but it's clearly having the opposite effect.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Um, my mother actually used to joke that even back in elementary school, I taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this recent family change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your relationships with friends or making it hard for you to make and keep friends?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say so, no. I mean... I haven't really noticed it being an issue with my friends. The problems are mostly just at work and at home, like I was saying. But as far as making or keeping friends... no, it doesn't really seem to get in the way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um, like I was saying earlier, it's taking a pretty big toll on everything. \n\nWhen things get to the point at work where you're actually put on a corrective plan... that's incredibly stressful. And then at home, having my spouse tell me that they have to manage my energy on top of all our normal caregiving routines... it just feels terrible. So, yes, my quality of life is definitely suffering. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. Those are definitely where it causes the most problems. Like I said, being on that corrective plan at school, and then the strain it puts on my marriage. I can't really think of any other specific areas outside of those two. That's pretty much where it hits the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and constant activity are more related to an inability to stay still and calm, or to difficulties with focusing on one task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. Um... I guess I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It's hard to pull those two things apart. \n\nI just know that I can't seem to stay in one place or just relax. Whether that's because I can't focus on one task for very long, or if my body just physically can't sit still... I don't really know which one is driving the other. It just all kind of happens at once, so I couldn't really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are impacting your ability to enjoy leisure activities or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, just having quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me. If my spouse is trying to watch a show, or... or if my relative is resting, I just can't sit there quietly. I'll start narrating what I'm doing, or humming, or I'll just start moving things around the room. It usually keeps up until someone finally asks me to please settle down. So, yeah, enjoying any kind of quiet downtime or relaxing is pretty tough right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by your inability to control your constant activity and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel overwhelmed or distressed by it, no. I mean... it's definitely frustrating because of the problems it causes at work and with my wife, like I mentioned before. But the actual moving around itself doesn't distress me. \n\nIt's just kind of how I operate. I don't sit there feeling upset that I can't stop moving, I just... I just do it. It's more of an issue for the people around me than it is an overwhelming feeling for me personally. So, no, I wouldn't use the word distressed.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling upset or bothered by your constant need to be active and moving?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that's pretty much what I was just getting at. Um... no, I'm not really upset by the need to move itself. Like I said, it's just kind of how I'm wired. \n\nI don't feel bothered by being active. It's really just the friction it causes with my wife, or, you know, the issues it brings up at school with my job that get under my skin. But the actual moving? No, that doesn't bother me personally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant activity and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function effectively in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely is. I mean, at work—I teach special ed, and the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the school put me on a corrective plan. So that's a major issue right there. \n\nAnd at home, my wife says the caregiving routines are just harder now. She told me that people basically have to manage my energy on top of everything else we're dealing with. It's hard to hear, because I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family... even though I’m honestly just trying to help out. So, yes, it's definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you get distracted or lose focus?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's a problem for me. I don't really lose focus or get distracted like that. \n\nWhen I have a task to do, or instructions to follow, I get it done. The issue isn't really finishing things... it's just that I'm constantly moving or pacing while I do them. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. It’s like I finish one thing—whether it's at school with my students or just stuff around the house—and I immediately jump right into the next thing. I just can't ever seem to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still or feel restless when you're supposed to be staying in one place?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I have to sit through an IEP meeting for one of my students, or if I'm in one of my admin classes, I just can't stay still. My leg will be bouncing under the table, or I'm clicking a pen... just shifting around in my chair constantly. I actually do it so much that people will glance over at me, and half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until I see them looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. Um... I mean, I know I'm always moving and can't sit still, but I don't really make snap decisions or do things without thinking them through first. I haven't really noticed that being an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty thorough with my work, especially with my students' paperwork. I don't really struggle with missing details or making careless mistakes like that. My issue is more just the constant need to be moving, not so much my focus or attention to the actual work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It happens a lot at work. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting, and I always tell myself I'm going to be brief. But then... I just keep going. I'll hear myself giving, you know, three different examples, a story about something that happened in my classroom, and just throwing out ideas that nobody even asked for. And I can see that the other person is clearly trying to wrap things up, but I just talk right through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes quickly or frequently?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My mood is pretty stable, I'd say. Um, I mean, I get stressed or frustrated sometimes just from the job, but I don't have sudden mood swings or quick changes like that. I'm generally pretty even-keeled emotionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, working in a special ed classroom, there's always a lot going on—kids making noise, things moving around, constant activity. But it doesn't really pull my focus away. I can tune it out pretty well. \n\nIt's more... well, like I mentioned before, my issue is really just my own motor constantly running, rather than getting sidetracked by whatever is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's causing major problems, honestly. At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that administration actually put me on a corrective plan. And at home... well, my spouse says that our normal caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else that needs to get done. \n\nIt's tough to admit, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help them out, but it's clearly having the opposite effect.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Um, my mother actually used to joke that even back in elementary school, I taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or... you know, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was a part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this recent family change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your relationships with friends or making it hard for you to make and keep friends?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say so, no. I mean... I haven't really noticed it being an issue with my friends. The problems are mostly just at work and at home, like I was saying. But as far as making or keeping friends... no, it doesn't really seem to get in the way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um, like I was saying earlier, it's taking a pretty big toll on everything. \n\nWhen things get to the point at work where you're actually put on a corrective plan... that's incredibly stressful. And then at home, having my spouse tell me that they have to manage my energy on top of all our normal caregiving routines... it just feels terrible. So, yes, my quality of life is definitely suffering. I go into these situations just trying to help out, but knowing I'm actually damaging trust with my colleagues and my family instead... it makes everyday life pretty difficult to navigate right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific areas of your life where you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are causing the most problems?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's really just the two areas I was just talking about... work and home. Those are definitely where it causes the most problems. Like I said, being on that corrective plan at school, and then the strain it puts on my marriage. I can't really think of any other specific areas outside of those two. That's pretty much where it hits the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and constant activity are more related to an inability to stay still and calm, or to difficulties with focusing on one task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. Um... I guess I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It's hard to pull those two things apart. \n\nI just know that I can't seem to stay in one place or just relax. Whether that's because I can't focus on one task for very long, or if my body just physically can't sit still... I don't really know which one is driving the other. It just all kind of happens at once, so I couldn't really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and restlessness are impacting your ability to enjoy leisure activities or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, just having quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me. If my spouse is trying to watch a show, or... or if my relative is resting, I just can't sit there quietly. I'll start narrating what I'm doing, or humming, or I'll just start moving things around the room. It usually keeps up until someone finally asks me to please settle down. So, yeah, enjoying any kind of quiet downtime or relaxing is pretty tough right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by your inability to control your constant activity and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel overwhelmed or distressed by it, no. I mean... it's definitely frustrating because of the problems it causes at work and with my wife, like I mentioned before. But the actual moving around itself doesn't distress me. \n\nIt's just kind of how I operate. I don't sit there feeling upset that I can't stop moving, I just... I just do it. It's more of an issue for the people around me than it is an overwhelming feeling for me personally. So, no, I wouldn't use the word distressed.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling upset or bothered by your constant need to be active and moving?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that's pretty much what I was just getting at. Um... no, I'm not really upset by the need to move itself. Like I said, it's just kind of how I'm wired. \n\nI don't feel bothered by being active. It's really just the friction it causes with my wife, or, you know, the issues it brings up at school with my job that get under my skin. But the actual moving? No, that doesn't bother me personally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant activity and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function effectively in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely is. I mean, at work—I teach special ed, and the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the school put me on a corrective plan. So that's a major issue right there. \n\nAnd at home, my wife says the caregiving routines are just harder now. She told me that people basically have to manage my energy on top of everything else we're dealing with. It's hard to hear, because I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family... even though I’m honestly just trying to help out. So, yes, it's definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you get distracted or lose focus?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's a problem for me. I don't really lose focus or get distracted like that. \n\nWhen I have a task to do, or instructions to follow, I get it done. The issue isn't really finishing things... it's just that I'm constantly moving or pacing while I do them. So, no, getting distracted and leaving things unfinished isn't really something I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of constant activity and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: It's been consistent my whole adult life, yes. But honestly, it goes back way further than that. Um... my mother used to joke that even back in elementary school, I basically taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. \n\nSo... yeah, this has been a part of who I am long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started up recently with all the family changes we've been going through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But honestly, it goes back way further than that. Um... my mother used to joke that even back in elementary school, I basically taught the class from my seat. I was just always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. \n\nSo... yeah, this has been a part of who I am long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started up recently with all the family changes we've been going through.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced symptoms of constant activity and restlessness in multiple settings, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it's everywhere. Like I said, it's at work with the school putting me on that corrective plan because I can't sit still or stop interrupting. It's at home, where my wife is frustrated because my pacing and constant moving make everything harder. And it's definitely in social situations, too. If we go out to dinner or have people over, I'm the one getting up every five minutes, clearing plates before people are finished, or just standing up while everyone else is sitting and talking. It doesn't matter where I am, I just can't seem to stop moving.
